| 4 | Verdict signing (the deferred trust-chain item from cycle 3): optional ed25519 signing of `.cw-release/review-<sha>.verdict`, so CI/hooks can tell a real reviewer verdict from a hand-written one — the gap flagged as "CI cannot tell a hand-written verdict from a real one" in the original audit. New `scripts/verdict-keygen.js` (mirrors `cw-attest-keygen.js`'s pattern but a separate trust domain/keypair) and `scripts/verify-verdict-signature.js` (portable node CLI, exit 0/1, shared by CI and the local hook). `release-flow.js`'s `signVerdictIfConfigured()` signs the verdict's exact on-disk bytes right after `verifyVerdict()` confirms a genuine `APPROVED <HEAD>`, IF `CW_RELEASE_VERDICT_PRIVKEY` is set — a true no-op otherwise (opt-in), and fails closed (`die()`) on a misconfigured or non-ed25519 key rather than silently emitting an unsigned verdict. `cut()` adds the `.sig` sidecar to the same commit as the verdict. `release-gate.yml`, `npm-publish.yml`'s dispatch check, and `block-unapproved-tag.sh` all now additionally require a valid signature once `.cw-release/verdict-signing.pub` is committed to the repo — before that, every check stays exactly grep-only (backward compatible). Built with a 5-dimension adversarial-review Workflow (crypto correctness, backward compatibility, YAML/shell correctness, fail-closed audit, test-coverage gaps) that found and got 6 real bugs fixed before merge: (1+2) both RELEASE.md's own setup snippet and `verdict-keygen.js`'s printed instructions committed the pubkey to `plugins/cool-workflow/.cw-release/` instead of the true repo root (same class of bug as the npm-publish.yml working-directory fix in cycle 1/#374, this time in the docs/tooling) — fixed by anchoring both on `git rev-parse --show-toplevel`; (3) `signVerdictIfConfigured` never checked the private key's algorithm — an RSA/EC key signed "successfully" but produced a signature that could never verify — added an `asymmetricKeyType === "ed25519"` check, fail closed; (4) the new `.sig` git-add line in `cut()` had zero static or dynamic test coverage (every `--cut` test case is `--dry-run` for safety, so it never reaches that line) — added a matching regex guard to `release-pipeline-hygiene-smoke.js`'s existing "Guard 1"; (5) the actual bash logic added to `release-gate.yml`/`npm-publish.yml` had no automated coverage at all (only manually verified during development) — new `verdict-signing-workflow-smoke.js` extracts the real `run:` block text straight out of both YAML files (never a hand copy) and executes it against constructed git fixtures for 6 scenarios each, including a loop-continuation proof; (6) `verify-verdict-signature.js`'s exit-code contract (2 for usage error, 1 for verification failure) was untested — pinned with 3 new assertions. The review ALSO surfaced a 7th, more severe finding — a P1, pre-existing (not introduced by this cycle) replay bug in the HEAD/HEAD~1 tolerance loop itself, where an attacker with tag-push access can replay an old validly-signed verdict onto an unreviewed new commit — confirmed by independent reproduction; surfaced to the user, who chose to scope a fix in a dedicated follow-up cycle rather than fold it into this one. | `plugins/cool-workflow/scripts/verdict-keygen.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/scripts/verify-verdict-signature.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/scripts/release-flow.js`, `.github/workflows/release-gate.yml`, `.github/workflows/npm-publish.yml`, `plugins/cool-workflow/scripts/block-unapproved-tag.sh`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/release-flow-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/block-unapproved-tag-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/release-pipeline-hygiene-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/verdict-signing-workflow-smoke.js` (new), `RELEASE.md` | Full smoke suite 183/183 (incl. new `verdict-signing-workflow-smoke.js`'s 14 cases and extended `release-flow-smoke.js`/`block-unapproved-tag-smoke.js`); `test:unit` 153/153; conformance 104/104; 13-agent adversarial-review Workflow (5 reviewers + 8 independent verifiers) confirmed 8 findings (0 refuted), all fixable ones resolved | BUILD OK; dist fresh; `release:check --skip-tests` clean | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 5 | The P1 replay bug surfaced at the end of cycle 4 (HEAD/HEAD~1 tolerance loop trusts "a validly-signed verdict exists" without ever proving HEAD is really the deterministic bump cut() would have produced on top of it) — scoped and fixed in this same PR/branch (not yet merged) rather than a separate follow-up, since it was found while reviewing this exact feature. New `scripts/verify-bump-reproduction.sh`: reproduces the bump independently in a scratch `git worktree` at the approved parent (`npm install --no-package-lock --ignore-scripts`, `npm run bump:version`, `npm run sync:project-index`, stages EXACTLY like `cut()` does — `git add -u` + explicit verdict/.sig paths, never `-A`), then requires the resulting `git write-tree` to match the actual tagged commit's tree byte-for-byte; `release-gate.yml`/`npm-publish.yml` call it only when the matched verdict is at HEAD~1 (never the literal tag commit) AND a pubkey is committed (this protection is layered on TOP of signing, not a replacement — see below). Validated against this repo's REAL history (v0.2.2's real approved-parent/tagged pair reproduces to a byte-exact tree match; a constructed replay of that same real verdict onto a commit with an injected backdoor file is correctly rejected). Its own adversarial-review Workflow (5 dimensions, 16 agents) then found 2 more P1s and 9 more real bugs in THIS fix before it shipped: the sharpest was that the first implementation's date-pinning (an app-level `CW_PROJECT_INDEX_DATE` env var read by `sync-project-index.js`) only worked by calendar coincidence — the scratch worktree runs the APPROVED PARENT's OWN checked-out (older) copy of that script, which for every release ever cut has no idea the env var exists, so it silently falls back to real wall-clock `new Date()` and reproduction only "works" when run on the exact day of the original cut (confirmed: replaying it against v0.2.0 on a later day produced a tree mismatch whose only diff was that one date string, and the new-this-cycle `verify-bump-reproduction-smoke.js`'s own "real release reproduces" assertion for v0.2.2 was a ticking time bomb that would flip red the very next calendar day). Replaced entirely with `scripts/fake-date-for-reproduction.js`, a Node `--require` preload overriding the global `Date` constructor — transparent to ANY script version regardless of whether it knows the mechanism exists, since it intercepts at the runtime level before application code runs; re-validated against v0.2.0 (cut 2026-07-05) on 2026-07-08 and it now reproduces correctly, no longer a coincidence. Also fixed: a one-character `&&`-to-`;` edit could have silently flipped the fail-closed gate to fail-open (each step's exit code is now checked explicitly and immediately, not via a chained condition); switched `npm ci` to `npm install --no-package-lock` to match this repo's own established convention (avoids a lockfile-desync failure class `npm ci` is strict about); stopped fully swallowing `npm install`/`bump:version`/`sync:project-index` output so a transient infra hiccup is distinguishable from a genuine reproduction mismatch in the workflow log; found and fixed a second real bug the SAME review surfaced (the preload script's own path was derived from `$REPO_ROOT`, the git repo being verified, instead of the script's own on-disk location — coincide in ordinary use but not when a test harness points the script at a different git context); investigated and declined a proposed "pin the scratch worktree's remote URL" fix after confirming empirically that `git worktree add` already shares the real repo's config (the concern only existed in this cycle's own clone-based test methodology, fixed there instead, not in the shipped script). Manually confirmed a residual, low-risk test-coverage gap (no test exercises `bump:version` actually performing a REAL, non-no-op version bump) is in fact structurally unreachable by any real release today: `cut()` never passes `--content` to `bump:version`, so a genuine non-no-op bump already fails the content-surface gate in the REAL release flow before a tag could ever be created — confirmed by running it directly against a scratch copy of this repo's own HEAD. Left unresolved and clearly flagged (not a code gap): this whole protection, like signing itself, is dormant in production until an operator actually runs `verdict-keygen.js` and commits `.cw-release/verdict-signing.pub` — that step has not been taken in this repo yet. | `plugins/cool-workflow/scripts/verify-bump-reproduction.sh` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/scripts/fake-date-for-reproduction.js` (new), `.github/workflows/release-gate.yml`, `.github/workflows/npm-publish.yml`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/verify-bump-reproduction-smoke.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/test/verdict-signing-workflow-smoke.js` | Full smoke suite 184/184 (incl. new `verify-bump-reproduction-smoke.js` against real repo history and 6 new scenarios in `verdict-signing-workflow-smoke.js`); `test:unit` 153/153; conformance 104/104; a second 16-agent adversarial-review Workflow (5 reviewers + 11 independent verifiers) confirmed 11 findings (0 refuted) across two rounds, all addressed or explicitly documented as accepted/non-issues | BUILD OK; dist fresh; `release:check --skip-tests` clean | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 6 | Label MCP tool results that carry stored, worker/agent/operator-authored free text (a blackboard message, a review comment, a worker's own failure message, an approve/reject reason, a judge's rationale), so a model reading them can tell "this came from stored data" apart from "this came from cool-workflow itself." `src/mcp/server.ts` gets a small `UNTRUSTED_RESULT_FIELDS` table keyed by MCP tool name, naming the exact field path(s) in that tool's result that hold such text; the `tools/call` handler adds a SECOND `content` block naming those paths, and never touches `content[0]` (the real payload, still byte-for-byte the same as the CLI's `--json` output) — so the CLI/MCP parity gate (`scripts/parity-check.js`, which only ever reads `content[0]`) is untouched. A first pass covered 6 tools (`cw_blackboard_message_list`, `cw_comment_list`, `cw_blackboard_summary`, `cw_worker_list`, `cw_worker_show`, `cw_worker_fail`); a 4-dimension adversarial-review Workflow (18 agents: field-path accuracy, missed tools, parity-gate safety, mechanism/message quality) then found the first pass was far from complete — it confirmed 14 findings (0 refuted), the sharpest a P1: three "read run status" tools an agent would naturally call (`cw_workbench_view`, `cw_multi_agent_status`, `cw_review_status`) re-show the SAME stored text through a different field path, fully getting around the label for anyone who calls the summary tool instead of the narrow one. Traced and fixed every confirmed gap by hand (not just taking the review's word for it — each new field path was re-checked against the real handler code before adding it): added 16 more tools to the table (`cw_blackboard_summarize`, `cw_review_status`, `cw_feedback_list/show/collect/task/resolve`, `cw_multi_agent_failures`, `cw_multi_agent_status`, `cw_operator_status`, `cw_operator_report`, `cw_node_list/show/snapshot`, `cw_audit_judge`, `cw_workbench_view`); widened the label text so it says the text may come from a worker, agent, OR operator (a person using the CLI/MCP by hand), not just "a worker or agent," since review comments and approve/reject reasons can come from a human too; reversed an earlier "leave it out" call on `cw_blackboard_summarize` once the review showed its 77-char cut still leaves room for a full short attack line. Pinned all three to the exact same commit SHAs `npm-publish.yml` already uses for `actions/checkout@v7.0.0`/`actions/setup-node@v6.4.0`, with version comments — confirmed dependabot's `github-actions` ecosystem watcher already tracks and updates this pinned-hash-with-comment format correctly (it's what bumped `npm-publish.yml`'s own pin from v5.0.1 to v7.0.0 across the intervening cycles, per `git log`). | `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/workflows/release-gate.yml`, `.github/workflows/github-release.yml` | YAML validated for all 4 workflow files; no runtime code touched (no build/test impact) | BUILD OK; dist fresh; `release:check --skip-tests` clean | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 8 | Added the two scanning layers the original audit flagged but deliberately deferred to the user (cycle 3's note: "left for the user to decide rather than defaulted in"): CodeQL static analysis and gitleaks secret scanning. The user asked for both, so this consciously ends the repo's "zero-third-party-actions" property (until now only `actions/checkout`/`actions/setup-node` were used anywhere in `.github/workflows/`) — `gitleaks/gitleaks-action` is the first non-GitHub-authored action added. Two decisions confirmed with the user first: both are informational only (not required/blocking status checks — the noisy-first-run risk is real on a repo with a single collaborator and no bypass path), and gitleaks scans full git history every run (the tool's own default), not just each diff. As due diligence before shipping, ran a one-time local full-history scan (`gitleaks detect --source . --log-opts="--all"`) against this real repo: 496 commits scanned, 0 leaks found — the real CI run should come back clean. New `.github/workflows/codeql.yml` (`github/codeql-action/init`+`analyze`, `languages: javascript-typescript`, `build-mode: none`, plus a weekly `schedule:` beyond what was asked, flagged plainly since new query packs can still catch existing code) and `.github/workflows/gitleaks.yml` (`gitleaks/gitleaks-action`, `fetch-depth: 0` for full-history scanning), both SHA-pinned with the same `@<sha> # vX.Y.Z` style used everywhere else, both with a workflow-level `permissions:` block and a `concurrency:` group matching `ci.yml`. A focused adversarial review (not the full multi-agent pass — this is a bounded, well-scoped CI addition) caught one real, must-fix bug before this shipped: `gitleaks.yml`'s first draft declared only `contents: read`, but the reviewer read gitleaks-action's actual v3.0.0 source and found its PR-scan mode calls the GitHub API for the PR's commit range with no try/catch around that specific call — with an explicit `permissions:` block present, the unlisted `pull-requests` scope defaults to `none`, so every `pull_request` run would 403-crash instead of reporting a clean pass/fail (a false failure on every PR, not an informational check). Fixed by adding `pull-requests: read`. Also dropped `codeql.yml`'s `actions: read` (verified via `gh api` that this repo is public, and GitHub's own template says that scope is only needed for private repos) rather than keep an unneeded grant with a comment misdescribing why it was there. | `.github/workflows/codeql.yml` (new), `.github/workflows/gitleaks.yml` (new) | YAML validated for both new files; `npm run release:check` full run (not `--skip-tests`) clean, confirming the new root-level workflow files don't trip any existing gate; local full-history gitleaks scan of the real repo, 0 leaks; a focused adversarial-review pass (permissions minimality, SHA-pin-to-tag verification via live `gh api` calls against the real upstream repos, `build-mode: none` correctness for a TS codebase, confirmed nothing makes either check a required/blocking gate) found and fixed the `pull-requests: read` gap above | BUILD OK; dist unaffected (no `plugins/cool-workflow/` file touched); real CI run to be watched after push, before calling either workflow done | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |

## Batch — post-v0.2.2 robustness hardening

> A four-agent robustness audit of v0.2.2 (state durability/crash consistency,
> external-process/error-path handling, input validation/scale limits,
> environment/upgrade robustness) turned up 3 P1s, 6 P2s, and a handful of P3s.
> Fixing the P1s and P2s in priority order, one capability per cycle, PR each,
> user reviews and merges (established convention for this repo — the user
> merges, not the agent).

| cycle | goal | files | tests | gate | tagged |
|-------|------|-------|-------|------|--------|
| 1 | (P1) A state.json reduced to `{}` (or missing both `workflow` and `paths`) was silently accepted as a healthy, fresh, empty run — `normalizeRunState` backfills every missing field as a recorded "change" with zero errors, so `cw status`/`cw resume` on a run whose state.json got corrupted or truncated to nothing showed a clean empty run instead of any warning, and the next checkpoint save cemented the data loss. Reproduced live: a 6-task run's state.json replaced with `{}` loaded and reported "normalized" with no errors. Fix, split across the core/shell purity boundary this codebase enforces (`migrations.ts` stays pure, no fs): `migrateRunState`/`reverseRunState` now compute a pure `report.suspectedDataLoss` flag, and the shell layer (`run-store.ts`'s `loadRunFromCwd`, the one place state.json is read) corroborates it against disk: if the run dir already has real content next to a `suspectedDataLoss` state.json, it refuses to load with a clear "this looks like state.json was corrupted, not a new run" error instead of silently returning it. A run dir with genuinely nothing else in it (state.json is all there ever was) still loads — indistinguishable from "nothing happened yet," and refusing it would be a false failure on legitimate early-lifecycle runs. A 12-agent adversarial-review Workflow (2 dimensions, verified against the actual commit via an isolated scratch checkout since the shared worktree had moved to another branch mid-session) confirmed 9 findings, two of them real must-fixes: the original signal was `NEITHER workflow NOR paths present` — an AND-of-negations that a partial wipe leaving just ONE of the two keys (even as `{}`) sailed straight through, never reaching the disk corroboration at all; and `RunRegistry.loadRun` (`run-registry-io.ts`, backing `cw run resume`/`cw run rerun`) read state.json directly via `loadRunStateFile`, completely bypassing `loadRunFromCwd`'s new guard. Fixed the first to `EITHER workflow OR paths missing` (only BOTH present clears the flag), and the second by extracting the guard into an exported `assertNotSuspectedDataLoss(runId, result)` in `run-store.ts`, called from both `loadRunFromCwd` and `RunRegistry.loadRun`. Also fixed a should-fix (`reverseRunState` never computed the signal, hardcoded `false` — now shares `computeSuspectedDataLoss` with `migrateRunState`) and a nit (dot-prefixed filesystem debris, e.g. a stray `.DS_Store`, could count as "real content" — now ignored). The review's OTHER should-fix — broaden `hasPreexistingRunContent` from `tasksDir`/`commitsDir`/the audit log to also check `resultsDir`/`dispatchesDir`/`artifactsDir`/`workersDir`/`candidatesDir`/`feedbackDir`/`stateNodesDir`/`blackboardDir`/`multiAgentDir`/`topologiesDir`/`report.md` — was implemented, then caught by the FULL test:gate (not the new unit test alone) as a real regression and reverted: `test/run-fixture-compat-smoke.js`'s real legacy fixtures (all 6 lack `paths`; `pre-app-simple-run` lacks `workflow` too — genuine historical shape, not corruption) failed on `cw graph` after `cw status` had already run against the same temp run dir, because `cw status` itself writes `audit/summary.json`+`audit/index.json` (`summarizeTrustAudit`) and creates empty `blackboard`/`candidates`/`topologies` directories as a side effect of a plain READ — the broadened check treated cw's own cache/derived writes as "real content" and refused a perfectly healthy, merely-already-viewed run on its SECOND read. Reverted `hasPreexistingRunContent` back to the narrow, empirically-verified-safe set (`tasksDir`/`commitsDir`/non-empty audit event log only — confirmed via direct reproduction that a plain `cw status` read never populates these three, only the derived/cache paths); a new regression-pin test drives two real `cw status` reads back to back on a legacy no-`paths` run and asserts both succeed even after the cache files exist. Lesson applied plainly: a plausible-sounding review suggestion still needs empirical verification against the REAL test corpus, not just internal logical consistency — the full `test:gate` run (not just the new unit test) is what caught this, underscoring why every cycle in this batch runs the full unsampled suite before it's considered done. Left for a dedicated follow-up cycle (already planned as this batch's cycle 2): `RunRegistry.locate`/`deriveRecord` (backing `cw run show`/`archive`) still has NO suspected-data-loss guard at all — only `loadRun` (resume/rerun) got it in this cycle, since that was the review's specifically confirmed finding. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/core/state/migrations.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/run-store.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/run-registry-io.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/statecore-suspected-data-loss.test.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/test/audit-verify-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/trust-audit-anchor-smoke.js` | `statecore-suspected-data-loss.test.js`: `{}` flagged; workflow-only or paths-only (not both) still flagged; both-present clears the flag; `reverseRunState` computes the same signal; a wiped state.json next to real task content, commit content (alone), or a non-empty audit log is refused; an empty run dir, and one with every content sub-dir (incl. ones deliberately NOT checked) pre-created but still empty, both still load; dot-prefixed debris alone doesn't trigger refusal; an end-to-end `cw status <run-id>` spawn confirms exit 1 + the CLI's `cw:` error format; a regression pin runs `cw status` twice on a legacy no-`paths` run and asserts the second read (now with cache files present) still succeeds. Full unsampled `test:gate` 185/185 passed (including `run-fixture-compat-smoke.js`'s 6 real legacy fixtures — this is the exact test that caught the broadened-directory-check regression before it shipped), plus 8 registry/resume/rerun/export smokes re-run individually to confirm `RunRegistry.loadRun`'s new guard didn't regress any existing resume/rerun/export path | BUILD OK; `npm run check` clean; dist fresh | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 8 | (P1) The local and container execution backends passed a possibly-`undefined` `request.timeoutMs` straight to `spawnSync`'s `timeout` option with no fallback — unlike the agent backend, which already falls back to a 600000ms default in three places (`execution-backend/agent.ts`). An unset timeout means Node's `spawnSync` never enforces one at all, so a hung node/shell/bun/docker/podman child (e.g. a task that reads from stdin and blocks, or a genuinely wedged process) blocks `cw drive` forever with no kill path — `spawnSync` is synchronous, so not even Ctrl-C reaches the child until the parent's own event loop resumes. Fixed both `executeLocal` (`local.ts`) and `runContainer` (`container.ts`) to fall back to the same 600000ms default the agent backend uses, with a comment explaining why an unset timeout can't mean "no timeout." A focused adversarial review of this fix (2 dimensions, 5 agents) then found the exact same unset-timeout bug class still open one file over: `remote-source.ts`'s `listArchive`, `declaredUncompressedSize`'s `unzip -l` call, and the real `unzip`/`tar` extraction all ran with no timeout at all, on downloaded/attacker-influenced archive bytes (reachable via `--link <url>`/a remote archive URL) — while that same file's own `git()` helper already falls back to 120000ms. Confirmed real (not refuted) and folded in: all four call sites now carry the same `timeoutMs || 120000` fallback `git()` already uses. Two other findings were reviewed and left as-is: `remote.ts`/`ci.ts`'s existing 120000ms default vs. this fix's 600000ms is a real inconsistency across backends but not a bug (both already had SOME timeout); `onramp.ts`'s `gitLines()` having no timeout/no `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` is real but out of scope for an execution-backend-timeout fix (a doctor/gate-path git helper, not a task-execution backend) — left for a future cycle. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/execution-backend/local.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/execution-backend/container.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/remote-source.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/execution-backends-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/remote-link-archive-smoke.js` | New section 6 in `execution-backends-smoke.js`: a real child given an explicit 150ms `timeoutMs` and a 5s sleep is killed (`status: failed`, attestation notes carry `ETIMEDOUT`), proving the timeout plumbing reaches `spawnSync`; a regression-pin check on the compiled `dist/shell/execution-backend/{local,container}.js` confirms the exact `|| 600000` fallback literal is present (the full 600s wait itself is infeasible to run live in a unit test). `remote-link-archive-smoke.js` gets the same style of regression pin for the 4 new `remote-source.js` timeout sites (a real hang is likewise infeasible to force deterministically on a tiny test archive). Full `execution-backends-smoke.js`, `remote-link-archive-smoke.js`, + 3 related backend smokes pass | BUILD OK; `npm run check` clean; dist fresh | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 9 | (P1) Neither the MCP `tools/call` result (`mcp/server.ts`, `content[0].text`) nor the CLI's `--json` output (`cli/io.ts`'s `printJson`) capped the size of the `JSON.stringify`'d payload. An aggregate/dashboard capability (`cw_workbench_view`, `cw comment list --json` on a run with thousands of comments, `cw_node_list`) can grow to hundreds of MB on a large run — a useless dump for a calling agent's context window even in the best case, and a raw `RangeError: Invalid string length` once the string crosses V8's per-string ceiling (~256M UTF-16 code units) in the worst case (both surfaces already catch and cleanly report a thrown error — `cli/entry.ts`'s top-level catch, `mcp/server.ts`'s `tools/call` try/catch — so this was never a hard process crash, just a cryptic message or an unusably huge payload instead of a clear, actionable one). New shared `core/format/safe-json.ts`: `safeJsonStringify(value, maxBytes = 10MB)` tries `JSON.stringify(value, null, 2)` and, if the result exceeds the cap (or the attempt itself throws), returns a small valid-JSON overflow notice (`error: "result-too-large"`, the cap, the actual size when known, a hint to narrow the query) instead of the real payload — a result under the cap is byte-identical to plain `JSON.stringify`, so this is invisible to the CLI/MCP parity gate's own (normal-sized) fixtures. Wired into both `cli/io.ts`'s `printJson` and `mcp/server.ts`'s `tools/call` content[0] construction — the same shared function, so a result that overflows still overflows identically on both surfaces, preserving CLI/MCP parity even in the overflow case. A 10-agent adversarial-review Workflow (2 dimensions) confirmed 2 real must-fix bugs in the first draft, both live-reproduced against the actual dist build before being trusted: (1) `JSON.stringify` returns the literal value `undefined` (not a string, despite its misleading `string` return-type declaration) for `undefined`, a function, or a Symbol — never throws for those — but the byte-length measurement ran OUTSIDE the try/catch on the assumption `text` was always a real string, so `safeJsonStringify(undefined)` threw a `TypeError` from `Buffer.byteLength`; `mcp/server.ts` calls it unconditionally (unlike `cli/dispatch.ts`, which already guards with `result.json !== undefined` before calling `printJson`), so any MCP tool handler that legitimately returns `undefined` would have turned a previously-harmless quirk (the old code just silently produced `text: undefined`, which `JSON.stringify` drops as an omitted key) into a thrown, request-failing error. Fixed with an explicit `typeof text !== "string"` pass-through, preserving the exact pre-existing behavior for this edge case. (2) The overflow notice's OWN size was never bounded — its `detail` field embeds a caught error's message verbatim with no length limit, so an error wrapping a large message (reproduced live: a 5MB message via a `toJSON()` that throws) produced a multi-MB "capped" notice, defeating the entire point, and in the extreme a large enough embedded message made the notice-building `JSON.stringify` call itself throw `RangeError: Invalid string length`, UNCAUGHT (no nested try/catch around it). Fixed by truncating `detail` to 500 characters before it ever reaches the notice, bounding the notice's own size regardless of the underlying error — confirmed the same 5MB-message case now produces a notice under 2KB. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/core/format/safe-json.ts` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/src/cli/io.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/mcp/server.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/safe-json-output-cap.test.js` (new) | `safe-json-output-cap.test.js`: under-cap is byte-identical to plain `JSON.stringify`; over an explicit small cap returns a valid overflow notice with the real size and never leaks the oversized payload text; exactly-at-cap passes through unchanged (off-by-one check); an 11MB value overflows the REAL default 10MB constant (not just a mocked cap); a circular reference (a cheap stand-in for "stringify itself throws," the real trigger being a value large enough to hit V8's ceiling) still yields a clean overflow notice; `undefined`/a function/a Symbol all pass through unchanged rather than throwing; a 5MB error message embedded via a throwing `toJSON()` still produces a notice under 2KB with a "truncated" marker; a compiled-`dist` regression pin confirms both `cli/io.js` and `mcp/server.js` actually call `safeJsonStringify`. `npm run purity:check` clean (Buffer usage in `core/` already has precedent in `core/trust/telemetry-attestation.ts`). Full unsampled `test:gate` 185/185 (incl. `cli-jsonmode-parity-smoke.js`, `mcp-surface-registry-smoke.js`, `mcp-untrusted-content-advisory-smoke.js`, `multi-agent-cli-mcp-surface-smoke.js`) | BUILD OK; `npm run check` clean; `purity:check` clean; dist fresh | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 2 | (P2) `RunRegistry.locate`/`deriveRecord` (`run-registry-io.ts`, backing `cw run show`/`archive`/`rerun` via `locate()`) swallowed EVERY reason a run's state.json failed to load — invalid JSON, an unsupported/future schemaVersion, or (as of cycle 1) suspected data loss — into the same silent `null`/"not found" outcome as a genuinely absent run, exactly the gap cycle 1's own review flagged as still open (`loadRun`, backing resume/rerun, got the suspected-data-loss guard in cycle 1; `locate`/`deriveRecord`, backing show/archive/rerun's lookup, did not). Fixed at two levels: `deriveRecord` (used by BOTH the single-run lookup path and the bulk-scan path that lists many runs at once) now also calls `assertNotSuspectedDataLoss` inside its existing try/catch, so a suspected-data-loss run is treated the same as corrupt/unsupported for bulk scans — silently excluded, one bad run never breaks a listing of everything else. `deriveRecordForRun` (the single-run lookup `locate()` actually calls) now, when `deriveRecord` returns null despite state.json existing, re-derives WITHOUT swallowing: a genuine JSON parse error propagates `readJson`'s own clear "Invalid JSON in ..." message untouched; an unsupported schema throws a distinct "Run state for `<id>` is corrupt (fail closed): ..." error; a suspected-data-loss run throws the cycle-1 "Refusing to load run..." message. A genuinely missing run (state.json never existed) is unchanged — still resolves to `undefined`/"not found", exactly as before. A 9-agent adversarial-review Workflow (2 dimensions) confirmed 2 real findings in the first draft: (must-fix) `deriveRecordForRun`'s new fallback re-read was itself unguarded — a run whose state.json is deleted by a concurrent process strictly between `deriveRecord`'s internal read and this fallback re-read made `readJson` throw a raw, uncaught "File not found: `<path>`" straight out of `locate()`/`showRun()`/`resume()`/`archive()`/`rerun()`, a scarier and materially different outcome than the "not found" the exact same underlying condition (the run doesn't exist) gets everywhere else, purely from timing; fixed by wrapping the fallback in try/catch and checking `fs.existsSync` again inside the catch — if the file is genuinely gone by then, resolve to `null` (not found) like everywhere else; only a real, still-present corruption rethrows. (should-fix) `archive()`'s direct `this.deriveRecord(repo, located.record.runDir)!` re-fetch (a pre-existing narrow TOCTOU pattern) was untouched by this cycle's new distinguishing logic, and `assertNotSuspectedDataLoss` inside `deriveRecord` gave it a brand-new way to return `null` — widening the crash surface for a run that becomes suspected-data-loss in the window between `archive()`'s `locate()` call and this re-fetch, from a silent mis-report (pre-existing) to a raw `TypeError: Cannot read properties of null` (new); fixed by routing through `deriveRecordForRun` (the same distinguishing logic every other single-run lookup uses) instead of a bare `deriveRecord(...)!`, with a clear thrown message on a genuine `null`. Both fixes verified with live race repros (monkey-patching `deriveRecord`/`locate` to inject the exact race window each finding described) before and after the fix. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/run-registry-io.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/registry-corrupt-state-distinct-error-smoke.js` (new) | `registry-corrupt-state-distinct-error-smoke.js`: missing run stays "not found"; invalid JSON surfaces the real parse error; a future schemaVersion surfaces a distinct corrupt-state error; a wiped state.json next to real task content is refused; bulk `search()` stays tolerant (2 healthy runs list fine, the 3 broken ones are silently excluded, no throw); end-to-end `cw run show`/`cw run resume` on corrupt runs exit 1 with the specific reason, a healthy run's `cw run show --json` is unaffected; a state.json deleted mid-lookup (race) resolves to not-found, not a raw exception; `archive()` racing with a concurrent wipe throws a clear message, not a TypeError. Full unsampled `test:gate` 186/186 | BUILD OK; `npm run check` clean; dist fresh | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 3 | (P2) Two gaps the original audit named together: `cw doctor` had zero coverage of run-state/audit-log integrity (a truncated state.json or a torn-tail audit log was invisible to the one tool users are told to run when something seems wrong), and there was no way to recover a run whose trust-audit log crashed mid-append — `cw audit verify` failed closed forever on it with no repair path. Added both. `doctor.ts` gets two new READ-ONLY checks (never writes/repairs, matching its own "READ-ONLY" invariant): `run-state-integrity` scans every run under `<cwd>/.cw/runs`, dry-run loads each one, and warns naming the run id for an unsupported schema, a JSON parse failure, or suspected data loss (reusing cycle 1/2's `assertNotSuspectedDataLoss`); `audit-integrity` then calls `verifyTrustAudit` on every run that loaded cleanly and warns naming any run whose chain fails verification, with a fix hint pointing at the new repair command. New `repairTrustAuditTornTail` (`trust-audit.ts`) repairs a torn TRAILING write — the ONE corruption shape a crash mid-append can produce (`durableAppendFileSync` only ever adds bytes at the current end of file, so an interruption can only ever leave the LAST append incomplete, never touch earlier already-flushed lines). Fails closed (`outcome: "refused"`) rather than guessing when a NON-trailing line is also unparseable, or when removing the torn tail still leaves an unverifiable chain — both shapes point at tampering, not a crash, and this tool must never be usable to launder a genuinely forged chain. Exposed as `cw audit repair <run> [--write]` (dry-run by default, matching this codebase's `cw state check [--write]` convention) + MCP `cw_audit_repair`, wired through the normal capability-table/parity machinery (deferred from the payload-probe bootstrap alongside its sibling `audit.verify`, for the same reason: mutates durable state, needs a seeded fixture). `fs-atomic.ts`'s `writeJson` was refactored (behavior-preserving) to share its temp+fsync+rename core with a new `writeTextDurable`, so the repaired NDJSON log gets the exact same atomic-write guarantee as every JSON write in this codebase, not a hand-rolled copy. Also fixed, as a drive-by (root-caused while running this cycle's own tests repeatedly): two PRE-EXISTING environment-dependent flakes in `doctor-smoke.js` — both "no agent configured" test cases used ambient `process.env`/a partial env-clear that let a REAL agent CLI (`claude`/`codex`/`gemini`, all happen to be on `$PATH` in this environment) auto-detect and silently invert the test's premise; both now force `CW_NO_AUTO_AGENT=1`, matching the guard the "shape" test already used. An 8-agent adversarial-review Workflow (2 dimensions) then confirmed 2 real must-fix bugs in `repairTrustAuditTornTail` itself, both live-reproduced before trusting them: (1) the fast-path check ("does the file end in a newline?") was being used as a proxy for "nothing is corrupt," but it only says whether the LAST WRITE completed — if a torn run is RESUMED (another event appended right after the garbled remnant, with no separating newline of its own from `durableAppendFileSync`), the two merge into one unparseable line and the file ends in a newline again, so the fast-path falsely declared the log "clean" while a genuinely corrupt merged line sat right there, undetected, with no other path in the tool that would ever catch it. Fixed by removing the newline shortcut entirely: every line is now always actually parsed first; a "clean" verdict is only ever based on real parseability. (2) The post-removal recheck passed no truncation anchor to `verifyEventsChain`, so an attacker who deletes real historical events and leaves only a torn-looking fragment behind gets an empty (or drastically shortened) "good" chain that trivially "verifies" — repair would then confidently overwrite the log with that emptied content and report `outcome:"repaired"`, permanently destroying the only evidence the chain had been truncated (the exact "launder a genuinely forged chain" case the function's own doc comment said must never be possible). This is the same blind spot `verifyTrustAudit` itself already has and documents when no anchor is given — fixed the way that sibling function already solves it: `repairTrustAuditTornTail` and `cw audit repair`/`cw_audit_repair` now accept the SAME `--expect-head`/`--expect-count` anchor `cw audit verify` does (reusing its `anchorOption` helper, now parameterized so its error message names the actual command instead of always saying "audit verify"); given an anchor, a repair that would fall short of the expected count/head is refused, never written. Without an anchor the blind spot remains, explicitly documented as a pre-existing, accepted limitation matching `verifyTrustAudit`'s own model — not a new regression, and now at least escapable for a caller who captured one. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/doctor.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/trust-audit.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/audit-cli.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/fs-atomic.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/core/capability-data.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/wiring/capability-table/trust-ledger.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/wiring/capability-table/parity.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/doctor-audit-integrity-repair-smoke.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/test/doctor-smoke.js` | `doctor-audit-integrity-repair-smoke.js`: no runs => both checks ok; a healthy run with real audit events => both ok; an unsupported-schema run, and separately a suspected-data-loss run => `run-state-integrity` warns naming the run; a torn audit tail (state.json itself fine) => `audit-integrity` warns naming the run, fix hint mentions `cw audit repair`, and the log is confirmed byte-unchanged after the doctor run (read-only proof); `cw audit repair` dry-run reports without touching the file, `--write` actually repairs, a follow-up `cw audit verify` confirms clean; a tampered (not just torn) log is refused (exit 1), never touched even with `--write`; CLI/MCP payload parity for `cw_audit_repair`; a torn-then-RESUMED log (the merged-line case) is correctly detected and repaired, not falsely "clean"; deleted history behind a torn fragment is refused when a captured anchor doesn't match, with the file confirmed untouched even under `--write`. Full unsampled `test:gate` 187/187; `npm run purity:check` clean; `npm run parity:check` clean (audit.repair correctly deferred, registry now 240 capabilities/198 MCP tools) | BUILD OK; `npm run check` clean; `purity:check` clean; `parity:check` clean; dist fresh | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |
| 4 | (P2) `recordTrustAuditEvent` (`trust-audit.ts`) did a read-modify-append with no lock — read the current tail, compute `prevEventHash`/`eventHash` off it, then append — unlike every other read-modify-write in this codebase. Two processes recording events for the SAME run at once could both read the same tail, compute the same `prevEventHash`, and both append: a forked hash chain, which (unlike a torn trailing write) is not confined to the last line, so `repairTrustAuditTornTail` (cycle 3) cannot fix it — a fork fails `verifyTrustAudit` for good. Fixed by holding `withFileLock(audit.eventLogPath, ...)` over the read-tail + compute-hashes + append critical section (event CONSTRUCTION, e.g. `scrubMetadata`/`normalizeEvidence`, stays outside the lock — no reason to hold it any longer than the actual read-modify-write). `repairTrustAuditTornTail` now takes the SAME lock too, so a repair can never interleave with a live append either. Proven fails-before/passes-after: a new concurrency smoke spawns 6 real child processes each appending 15 events to the same run at once; reverting the lock (verified live, then restored) reproduces a badly forked chain (`trust-audit-chain-broken` on more than half the events); with the lock, all 90 events land with a fully verified, unforked chain, every time. An 8-agent adversarial-review Workflow (2 dimensions) then confirmed 2 more real bugs, both live-reproduced: (must-fix) `createEventId`'s event-count read (`readEventsRaw`, used to number ids like `audit-sandbox.path-0007`) ran BEFORE the lock, independently of the prevEventHash read inside it — so concurrent writers could read the same pre-lock count and mint the IDENTICAL id for two different, correctly-serialized-by-the-lock events. The hash chain itself was already correctly fork-proof (that part of the fix worked), but the id — referenced elsewhere as `auditEventIds`/`parentEventIds` for provenance across `commit.ts`/`coordinator-io.ts`/`candidate-scoring-io.ts` — was not unique, silently breaking the audit trail's identity guarantee; reproduced live at the review's own scale (10 workers x 25 events: 241 unique ids out of 250). Fixed by moving `id` generation INSIDE the lock, reusing the SAME `prior` array already read there for `prevEventHash` (one read now serves both, instead of two separate reads) — reconfirmed clean (250/250 unique ids) at the same 10x25 scale. The concurrency smoke, which only asserted `eventCount`/`verified`/`corruptLines` and had NOT caught this, now also asserts every event's id is unique. (should-fix, partially addressed) reading the full event log to parse every prior event, just to reach the last one's hash, now happens inside the lock, and lock-hold time scaling with total log size risks the documented 30s stale-lock-steal window on a very large audit log; reusing one read for both id and prevEventHash (the must-fix above) already halves this cost, but the remaining single full-log read+parse under the lock is a known, documented scaling limit for very large logs, not a full fix — an O(1)-per-append design (e.g. a persisted tail-hash+count cache) would need a larger, separate architectural change, deliberately not attempted in this cycle. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/trust-audit.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/trust-audit-append-lock-concurrency-smoke.js` (new) | `trust-audit-append-lock-concurrency-smoke.js`: 6 concurrent child processes x 15 events each => all 90 present, `verified: true`, `corruptLines: 0`, every event's id unique; confirmed the same test fails hard (dozens of `trust-audit-chain-broken` checks) when the lock is reverted, restored after confirming. | 6 | (P2) Several `shell/*.ts` call sites each rolled their own ad-hoc numeric-flag parse for `--limit` (and friends). Every one shared one of two bugs the audit named: `value === undefined ? undefined : Number(value)` let a BARE flag (parseargv turns a valueless `--limit` into boolean `true`) silently become `Number(true) === 1`, never an error; or a `value || fallback` pattern ALSO silently swallowed a genuinely-given `0` (and a `NaN` from a bad string, since `NaN` is falsy too), guessing in whichever direction was wrong for that call site — `nextDispatchTasks` (`core/pipeline/dispatch.ts`) treated a bare/garbage `--limit` as "use the configured default," while `multi-agent fanout`'s `concurrencyLimit` treated the same shape as "no limit at all" (fan out unbounded), the opposite failure mode. Added one shared parser, `requiredNumberFlag` (new `core/util/numeric-flag.ts`, following the `core/util/collate.ts` precedent for a pure cross-layer utility — it must live under `core/`, not `cli/io.ts`, since every call site is in `shell/**` and the purity gate forbids `shell/**` importing `cli/**`): absent stays absent (the caller's own default still applies); a bare flag or an unparseable value now THROWS with a clear message instead of guessing; a genuine `0` or a negative number is returned as-is, with clamping (if any) left to the call site. Wired into every site the audit named: `dispatch --limit` (`pipeline-cli.ts`, `dispatch.ts`'s `nextDispatchTasks` also switched its `limit || default || 4` to `limit ?? default ?? 4` plus a `Math.max(0, ...)` clamp before `.slice()`, since a negative end index means something different than "cap at N" to `Array.prototype.slice`), `sched lease --limit` and `sched policy set`'s `maxConcurrent`/`maxAttempts`/`leaseTtlMs`/`backoffBaseMs`/`backoffFactor`/`backoffCapMs` (`scheduling-io.ts` — its own local `numericFlag` helper now delegates to the shared parser, with the shared function's two error messages deliberately split and worded so the "unparseable value" case byte-matches the OLD helper's pre-existing wording exactly, so the existing `sched-policy-validation-smoke.js` regex assertion needed no change), `metrics summary --limit` (`metrics-cli.ts`), all 6 identical `run.*`/`queue.*`/`gc.*` call sites in `registry-cli.ts`, and multi-agent fanout's aliased `--limit`/`--concurrency`/`--concurrencyLimit` (`multi-agent-cli.ts` — scoped to only this one flag; the file's other 7 `numberArg(...)` call sites, e.g. mapperCount/judgeCount/debateRounds, were not named by the audit and were left unchanged). A follow-up 8-agent adversarial-review Workflow (2 dimensions: correctness of the parser/call-site integration, completeness of coverage) confirmed 6 real findings (2 refuted as non-issues), all fixed before considering this cycle done: (must-fix) `registry-cli.ts`'s `offset` fields in the SAME three functions the cycle touched (`runSearchCli`/`runListCli`/`historyCli`) sat one line below the fixed `limit` field with the IDENTICAL untouched bare-flag bug — a bare `--offset` silently made every existing run vanish from `run list`/`run search`/`history` output with no error, indistinguishable from "no runs exist"; now routed through `requiredNumberFlag`. (must-fix) `requiredNumberFlag` itself silently treated an empty or whitespace-only string (`Number("")`/`Number("   ")` are both `0`, reachable via `--flag=` or an unset shell variable in `--flag=$VAR`) as a genuine `0` instead of throwing — the same "unusable input, not a real value" case the function exists to catch; now rejected with the same `Invalid ... expected a number` message as any other unparseable value. (should-fix) `registry-cli.ts`'s OTHER numeric flags never named by the original audit (`queue add`'s `--priority`, `gc plan`/`gc run`'s `--reclaimAfterArchiveDays`, `run archive`'s `--older-than-days`, `orphans gc`'s `--minAgeMinutes`/`--min-age-minutes`) shared the identical bare-flag-becomes-1 bug ("and friends" turned out to mean more than just pagination limit) — all now routed through the shared parser; `run archive`'s pre-existing, DIFFERENT "Missing run id (or --older-than-days N...)" message for the case where NEITHER a run id nor the flag is given was deliberately preserved (only the flag's own bare/garbage handling changed). | 7 | (P2, last of this batch) No signal handler existed anywhere in `src/` for the parent `cw` process — a bare Ctrl-C (SIGINT) or an external SIGTERM ran the kernel default: instant termination, no `finally` anywhere got a chance to run. Landing mid-`withFileLock` (guarding `state.json`/the trust-audit log) left a stale `.lock` file — a ~6s hard failure on the very next command, well under the 30s stale-lock steal window; a SIGTERM sent only to the parent PID (a supervisor/CI cancel, as opposed to Ctrl-C's whole-process-group SIGINT) orphaned the in-flight agent child spawned via `spawnSync`, left running to its own timeout with no `cw` left to reconcile it. Fixed in `drive.ts`'s `drive()` — the single choke point every entry (`dispatch --drive`/`--step`, `run --drive`, `resume --drive`, and the MCP handlers, which all route through the same function): a SIGINT/SIGTERM handler is installed around the loop and removed in a `finally` on every call (this function recurses for nested sub-workflow runs, and many existing tests call it repeatedly in one process, so nothing must accumulate on `process`'s listener list across calls). The FIRST signal sets a flag checked only at the TOP of the next outer-loop iteration — never mid-step — so the in-flight step always finishes its own bookkeeping (checkpoint, phase-progress, commit) before the loop stops; the run then returns a normal `DriveResult` with `status: "blocked"` and a step reason naming the interrupting signal, resumable the exact same way the pre-existing max-iteration block already is (re-driving the same run id continues from where it stopped). A SECOND signal means the caller wants out right now, not a graceful stop, and force-exits immediately with the conventional `128+signum` code (130/143). Explicitly out of scope, both already-documented, pre-existing limits this cycle does not attempt to close: interrupting an already-in-flight `spawnSync` call itself (Node cannot dispatch a JS-level signal callback until a fully synchronous blocking call returns control to the event loop, so a signal arriving mid-spawn is only noticed once that call finishes on its own) and reaping the orphaned child on a parent-only SIGTERM (both would need switching the agent spawn path off `spawnSync` to an async spawn the parent can explicitly signal — a materially larger change, left as a documented follow-up). Proven deterministically, with no real OS signal race: a 3-sequential-task fixture app (`phase(...)` with `maxConcurrentAgents: 1`, giving exactly one outer-loop iteration per task) drives through a stub agent; monkeypatching the ONE shared `saveCheckpoint` call (invoked at least once per completed task across every code path — dispatch-fail, retry/park, cache-hit-accept, fresh-accept, sub-workflow accept, commit-terminal, concurrent-round flush) to synchronously call `process.emit(signal, signal)` on its first invocation reproduces the exact real-world timing (signal lands right after step 1 finishes, right before step 2 would start) without depending on OS scheduling. A follow-up 6-agent adversarial-review Workflow (2 dimensions: correctness of the signal-handling logic incl. nested/recursive drive() calls and listener-leak safety, and completeness — other places that might need stop-signal handling) confirmed 3 real findings (1 refuted as a non-issue), all fixed or covered before considering this cycle done: (must-fix) a signal landing on the run's OWN terminal commit step (the run is ALREADY fully done and committed at that exact point) still unconditionally pushed the "blocked" step and forced `exhaustedMaxIterations` false — `finalDriveStatus` then fell through to that pushed step's status and never reached its own `return "complete"`, so a genuinely-finished run reported `blocked`; live-reproduced (a 1-task run interrupted on its exact terminal `saveCheckpoint` call reported `blocked` with `completedWorkers === plannedWorkers` and a real `commitId` already present) and confirmed to have a concrete downstream effect (`pipeline-cli.ts`'s `--bundle` gate reads `status === "complete"` and would silently skip sealing a bundle for a run that in fact fully finished). Fixed by short-circuiting the interrupted-blocked push when `completedWorkers === plannedWorkers && committed` — the exact same "already done" check `finalDriveStatus`'s own `once` branch already uses — so the run's real last step (its terminal commit, `status: "complete"`) is what `lastStepStatus` sees instead. (should-fix) `scripts/children/batch-delegate-child.js` (the batch spawner `driveConcurrentRound`'s concurrent-round path uses for width>1 rounds) had no SIGINT/SIGTERM handler of its own, unlike its sibling `agent-adapter-core.js`; a direct kill of it (e.g. the parent's own `spawnSync` timeout, or a signal that reaches it but not its own spawned grandchildren) orphaned every still-running job — live-reproduced with a real grandchild left running (reparented to PID 1) after its parent was killed. Fixed by tracking every spawned child in a `Set` and forwarding the first stop signal as `SIGTERM` to all of them (a second signal escalates straight to `SIGKILL` and exits immediately), mirroring the per-job timeout escalation this file already used. (should-fix) the new smoke test only ever exercised `driveStep()`'s width=1 `saveCheckpoint` call site — `driveConcurrentRound`'s own once-per-round `saveCheckpoint` (the width>1 path this cycle's own fix touches) was never exercised by any signal test; closed with a new parallel-phase fixture. One finding was independently verified and REFUTED, left as-is: "the graceful-stop guarantee doesn't hold for concurrent/parallel-phase rounds" (a whole round's tasks still finish once a signal lands mid-round) — this is pre-existing, already-documented round-vs-task granularity (the SAME "step" terminology `--once` already used before this commit), not a new inconsistency this cycle introduced; now pinned by a regression test instead of left unverified. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/drive.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/scripts/children/batch-delegate-child.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/sigint-sigterm-drive-loop-smoke.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/test/sandbox-env-batch-hardening-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/docs/project-index.md` (smokeTests 190→191) | New `sigint-sigterm-drive-loop-smoke.js`: for both SIGINT and SIGTERM — exactly 1 of 3 sequential tasks completes before the interruption, the run reports `blocked` with a step reason naming the interrupting signal, the other 2 tasks are confirmed still `pending`, `process.listenerCount(signal)` is unchanged after both an interrupted call and a normal completed call (no listener leak), and re-driving the same run id resumes and finishes the remaining tasks; a real child-process spawn confirms a second rapid signal force-exits with code 130 (SIGINT) / 143 (SIGTERM) and that `drive()` never returns normally once the second signal fires. Extended after the review: an interrupt landing exactly on a 1-task run's own terminal commit checkpoint (discovered empirically per-run, not hardcoded) now asserts `status: "complete"` with a real `commitId`, not `blocked`; a 3-task parallel-phase (width=3) round confirms all 3 tasks still complete when signaled mid-round (pinning the accepted round-granularity limit) while the run still correctly reports `blocked` before its next round/commit and resumes to completion. `sandbox-env-batch-hardening-smoke.js` gained a new case: a real grandchild process, spawned through `batch-delegate-child.js`, is confirmed to actually stop (an alive-marker file stops growing) once the batch child itself is sent SIGTERM, not left orphaned. Full unsampled `test:gate` 191/191 (re-run clean after the review fixes); `test:unit` 155/155 unaffected; `npm run check`/`purity:check`/`parity:check` all clean | BUILD OK; `npm run check` clean; `purity:check` clean; `parity:check` clean; dist fresh | no (defensive hardening, no version bump) |

## Batch — post-v0.2.2 performance hardening

> A four-agent performance audit of v0.2.2 (CLI/MCP startup latency, filesystem
> I/O patterns, algorithmic/memory hot spots on large runs, and concurrency/
> process-spawn overhead) measured every finding live against this tree before
> reporting it — no estimates without a real benchmark. Findings were ranked
> into a P1 (high value, low risk) / P2 (high value, needs care) / P3 (needs a
> product decision — snapshot-file format, telemetry-ledger format, MCP
> request serialization) plan. Fixing P1 then P2 in order, one capability per
> cycle, same discipline as the robustness batch: implement, regression-pin
> the improvement (not just correctness), full unsampled gate, adversarial
> review, ITERATION_LOG row, PR — user reviews and merges.

| cycle | goal | files | tests | gate | tagged |
|-------|------|-------|-------|------|--------|
| 1 | (P1) Phase/task selection was O(tasks x taskIds-per-phase) per call — `run.tasks.filter((task) => phase.taskIds.includes(task.id))` re-scanned every task against a linear `.includes()` per phase — and this shape is called several times per drive hop (`firstRunnablePhase`/`updatePhaseStatuses` in `core/pipeline/dispatch.ts`, `selectDriveTask` in `core/pipeline/drive-decide.ts`, plus `emitPhaseProgress`/`maybeExpandLoop`'s three scans/`driveConcurrentRound`'s batch filter in `shell/drive.ts`, and `summarizePhases`/the phase-status table row in `shell/operator-ux.ts`/`shell/report.ts`), so a whole run's selection cost compounded to O(tasks^3) — the original audit measured a SINGLE `firstRunnablePhase` call taking 254-712ms at 5000 tasks. Fixed all 8 call sites the same way: convert the phase's (or, for the loop-phase union in `maybeExpandLoop`, the merged) `taskIds` array to a `Set` once before filtering, so membership is O(1) instead of O(taskIds.length). Purely a lookup-structure change — iteration order, and therefore every existing byte-exact output, is unchanged (`Array.prototype.filter` still walks `run.tasks` in the same order; only the membership test inside it got cheaper). **Review follow-up:** an adversarial review of the fix found the SAME array-scan-per-item shape still present at 4 sibling sites — `buildCompactGraphFromView`'s bucket-collapse filter (`core/state/state-explosion/graph.ts`), `deriveFailures`/`readyCommitCommand`'s role/membership/worker/selection lookups (`shell/multi-agent-operator-ux.ts`), `requireArtifactRefs`/`requireMessages` (`shell/coordinator-io.ts`), and `summarizeTopologies`' fanin filter (`shell/topology-io.ts`) — fixed the same way (a `Set` built once). Designing an honest regression test for these 4 surfaced 3 MORE independent, previously-unreviewed O(N^2) bugs in the exact same call paths, found only because proving the reviewed fix required a large enough N to see it: a bucket-building loop in `buildCompactGraphFromView` that rebuilt its accumulator array via spread (`[...(map.get(k) || []), id]`) on every item instead of `.push()`; 3 dedup helpers in `multi-agent-operator-ux.ts` (`unique`/`uniqueById`/`uniqueByFailure`) using `values.indexOf/findIndex(...) === index` (O(N) per item); and 3 row-builders in `trust-audit.ts` (`workerRows`/`candidateRows`/`commitRows`) doing an `array.find()` per id instead of a `Map` lookup. All fixed the same way (`Map`/`Set` built once, or `.push()` instead of spread). `requireArtifactRefs`/`requireMessages`'s own perf pin was left correctness-only: proving it needs a large `state.artifacts`, but at that scale a separate, much larger bug (`persistBlackboardState` rewriting every artifact/topic/context/snapshot/decision record to its own file on every single write — measured at 41s for one call with 200k pre-existing artifacts) swamps it completely; that bug is tracked as its own follow-up, out of scope here. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/core/pipeline/dispatch.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/core/pipeline/drive-decide.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/drive.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/operator-ux.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/report.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/core/state/state-explosion/graph.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/multi-agent-operator-ux.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/coordinator-io.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/topology-io.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/trust-audit.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/pipelinecore-phase-scan-performance.test.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/test/perf-review-followup-set-scans.test.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/docs/project-index.md` (smokeTests unchanged, one more new `.test.js`) | New `pipelinecore-phase-scan-performance.test.js`: correctness unchanged (the same phase/task is still selected on a 5000-task, 20-phase worst-case fixture where every earlier phase is fully completed, forcing the walk through all of them); then asserts 100 calls each to `firstRunnablePhase`/`updatePhaseStatuses`/`selectDriveTask` at that scale complete within a 3s combined budget — a bound the OLD O(tasks^2)-per-call code fails hard (confirmed live: reverting the fix to the original `.includes()` form pushed a single 100-call loop to 3398ms, over budget, while the fixed code finishes in low milliseconds). New `perf-review-followup-set-scans.test.js`: each of the 4 review-fix sites got its own, individually-measured budget after the first draft's single shared budget turned out to not discriminate at all (verified: it passed even with all 4 fixes reverted, because the DOMINANT cost at the chosen scale was one of the 3 newly-found bugs, not the reviewed fix). Per site, live-measured fixed vs. reverted: `buildCompactGraphFromView` at 64,000 same-kind nodes, 50ms fixed vs. 20,383ms reverted (budget 1000ms); `summarizeMultiAgentOperator` at 64,000 roles/memberships/workers, 344ms fixed vs. 4,578ms reverted AT ONLY 16,000 (budget 2000ms); `summarizeTopologies` at 1,500 records x 200 groupIds/fanoutIds each, 182ms fixed vs. 7,532ms reverted (budget 1500ms) — all 3 confirmed to fail the assertion when the corresponding fixes are reverted. `requireArtifactRefs`/`requireMessages`: correctness-only (single ref, several refs in one call, and a genuinely unknown ref), no perf budget, for the reason above. All pre-existing correctness unit tests for these functions pass unmodified. Full unsampled `test:gate` 191/191; `test:unit` 158/158; conformance 104/104; `npm run check`/`purity:check`/`parity:check` all clean | BUILD OK; `npm run check` clean; `purity:check` clean; `parity:check` clean; conformance 104/104; dist fresh | no (perf hardening, no version bump) |
| 2 | This batch's own cycle 1 named the exact follow-up: `persistBlackboardState` (`coordinator-io.ts`) and `persistTopologyState` (`topology-io.ts`) put back EVERY topic/context/artifact/snapshot/decision (or topology run) to its own file on every call, with no check for a change. With N records on hand, one call did N file writes; N such calls to build up to N records cost near N × N file writes in all — the same 41-second-at-200k-artifacts measurement cycle 1 cited. Found again independently while planning a speed test for cycle 1's `requireArtifactRefs`/`requireMessages` fix, since at large N it swamped that fix's own effect completely. Fixed with an id list that tracks which records were made or changed since the last call — kept OFF the state object itself (a `WeakMap` keyed by the state object, not a field on it), so it can never get into `state.json`'s bytes. Every place a record is made or changed marks its own id; `persistBlackboardState`/`persistTopologyState` now write only the ids in that list, then clear it. The hard part: two record changes (`context.decisionId`, `artifact.trustAuditEventIds`) happen AFTER an inner, nested `persistBlackboardState` call (inside `recordCoordinatorDecision`) has already written its own list and cleared it — missing a fresh mark there would silently drop that late change from disk. | 3 | (P1) `recordTrustAuditEvent` re-parsed the ENTIRE `events.jsonl` log on EVERY append just to learn two numbers: the prior event count (for the new event's id) and the last event's hash (for `prevEventHash`) — O(events) per append, O(events^2) over a run's whole life; a live audit measured ~80ms/append at 50k events. Fixed with a small sidecar tail cache (`tail-cache.json`, next to the log: `{schemaVersion, logBytes, count, lastHash}`) keyed on the log's OWN byte size — read under the same lock as the append. | 8 | (found by review, not part of this batch's own topic) A review of cycle 1's fix, above, turned up a real, separate bug in an EARLIER cycle: the SIGINT/SIGTERM code from cycle P2-6 (`drive()`'s `try {for(...)} finally {...}` shape in `shell/drive.ts`, commit dcf39a5). That code's own comment said a signal would be caught between rounds, and only missed a signal landing DURING one round's `spawnSync` call. This was not true. Node.js does not give the event loop a turn — so it does not run a JS `process.on(signal, ...)` listener — until ALL of a run of sync code stops, not just one round of it. Since `drive()`'s WHOLE multi-round loop never stops for anything (every round spawns its agent with `spawnSync`, and nothing between rounds ever does `await`), a real signal sent at ANY point in a live, multi-round `cw drive` run stays queued until the loop ends on its own — by which time the `finally` block has already taken its listener off, so the signal never reaches it. A user's Ctrl-C (or a job runner's SIGTERM) during a live run does nothing at all, not even the graceful stop the code was built to give. Proved live first, with a plain sync busy-loop (no `spawnSync` at all): a real, kernel-sent SIGINT — from a truly separate OS process running `kill -INT` — is dropped for the whole time the loop runs, then fires within ~1ms of the loop's own end, the FIRST point the event loop gets a turn. Fix: a new `driveAsync()` export in `shell/drive.ts`. It shares every other part of `drive()`'s code — pulled out as small named functions (`driveOneRound`, `createStopSignalController`, `finalizeDriveResult`, `buildDriveContext`, `createPhaseProgressEmitter`) so the two loops can never drift apart — but adds one new line: `await` a real, `setImmediate`-based turn of the event loop after each round, before the next round's sync work starts. This gives Node.js the one turn it needs to run the queued signal's listener and set the stop flag, which the loop then reads at the top of its next pass — the same "stop after the current round, never mid-round" shape the code already had, now made to work for real. `drive()` itself is UNCHANGED (same file, same behavior, byte for byte) — kept for the ~24 tests and the one recursive sub-workflow call site (`runSubWorkflow`) that call it today, and as the plain form any caller not needing real signal response can go on using. Wired `driveAsync()` into the LIVE entry points a real Ctrl-C/SIGTERM would actually reach: `runDriveStep`/`quickstartRun` (`shell/pipeline-cli.ts`), and `runResumeCli`'s `--drive` weld (`shell/registry-cli.ts`). Since the whole CLI path (`cli/entry.ts` -> `cli/dispatch.ts` -> the capability-table handler) ran fully sync end to end (no real `await` anywhere, even though `runCli` was already marked `async`), and the MCP server (`mcp/server.ts`) had no `async` code at all, both had to become `await`-aware: `dispatch()`/`dispatchTable()` now `await` each handler's result (a no-op for the ~199 rows still returning a plain value, since `await` on a plain value just moves on next tick); `mcp/server.ts`'s `handleRequest`/`handleLine` do the same, with a small promise chain in `startServer()` so replies still come back in the SAME order the requests came in. The one capability whose handler now gives back a real `Promise` (`run.drive.step`, reached by `cw run --drive`/`quickstart`/`cw run resume --drive`) needed its own call sites (`wiring/capability-table/pipeline.ts`, `wiring/capability-table/scheduling-registry.ts`) to `await` it too. A signal that lands while a NESTED sub-workflow's own (still sync) `drive()` call is running is still not caught until that nested call ends on its own — a known, accepted gap, the same shape as the already-accepted "cannot stop mid-`spawnSync`" limit, just one level of nesting deeper; closing that would mean turning the WHOLE recursive call graph async, a much bigger change left for a later cycle if it turns out to matter in real use. Two more real, unrelated bugs turned up while wiring this through, both fixed: (1) `captable-not-implemented-handler.test.js`'s broad sweep called every `mcp.handler` with no args inside a plain `try/catch`; `run.drive.step`'s now-`async` handler throws by REJECTING its returned `Promise`, not by a sync throw, so the sweep's sync `try/catch` missed it and Node's own unhandled-rejection crash took the whole test down — fixed by `await`ing each handler's result inside the SAME `try`, which now catches both shapes the same way. (2) `quickstart-bundle-smoke.js`'s own `clearAgentEnv()` never set `CW_NO_AUTO_AGENT=1` (the same gap this log already fixed once for `doctor-smoke.js`), so on a box with `claude`/`codex`/`gemini`/`opencode` on PATH, its "no agent configured" case silently ran a REAL agent CLI instead of staying blocked — found and fixed as a drive-by, unrelated to `driveAsync`. | `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/drive.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/pipeline-cli.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/shell/registry-cli.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/wiring/capability-table/pipeline.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/wiring/capability-table/scheduling-registry.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/cli/dispatch.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/cli/entry.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/core/capability-data.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/src/mcp/server.ts`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/drive-async-real-signal-smoke.js` (new), `plugins/cool-workflow/test/captable-not-implemented-handler.test.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/quickstart-bundle-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/quickstart-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/run-resume-drive-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/parallel-onramp-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/test/drive-concurrency-flag-smoke.js`, `plugins/cool-workflow/docs/project-index.md` (smokeTests 191->192) | New `drive-async-real-signal-smoke.js`: a REAL, kernel-sent SIGINT/SIGTERM — this test's OWN process running `process.kill(childPid, signal)` on a truly SEPARATE child process, never `process.emit()`, which never leaves the same process/tick and so proves nothing about real signal delivery — sent right after an on-disk marker shows round 1's agent child has truly started (so the signal lands while the driver is DEFINITELY still blocked inside `spawnSync`). Checked both ways on purpose, using the SAME real signal at the SAME timing: `driveAsync()` stops after round 1, reports `blocked` naming the signal, and a re-drive finishes the run; the plain `drive()` has NO reaction at all and drives all 3 tasks straight through — the exact contrast that proves the fix is real, not luck.
